written in Rust</a></li></ul><li><a href="#victims">Victims</a></li><li><a href="#attribution">Attribution</a></li><li><a href="#conclusion">Conclusion</a></li><li><a 												<img width="1200" height="600" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/24081845/bluenoroff-ghostcall-featured-image-1200x600.jpg" class="attachment-securelist-2020-thumbnail-large size-securelist-2020-thumbnail-large wp-post-image" alt="" decoding="async" loading="lazy" srcset="" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1200px) 100vw, 1200px" data-src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/24081845/bluenoroff-ghostcall-featured-image-1200x600.jpg" data-srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/24081845/bluenoroff-ghostcall-featured-image-1200x600.jpg 1200w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/24081845/bluenoroff-ghostcall-featured-image-300x150.jpg 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/24081845/bluenoroff-ghostcall-featured-image-1024x512.jpg 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/24081845/bluenoroff-ghostcall-featured-image-768x384.jpg 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/24081845/bluenoroff-ghostcall-featured-image-1536x768.jpg 1536w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/24081845/bluenoroff-ghostcall-featured-image-700x350.jpg 700w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/24081845/bluenoroff-ghostcall-featured-image-740x370.jpg 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/24081845/bluenoroff-ghostcall-featured-image-560x280.jpg 560w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/24081845/bluenoroff-ghostcall-featured-image-800x400.jpg 800w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/24081845/bluenoroff-ghostcall-featured-image.jpg 2000w" />											</figure>

											<div class="js-reading-content">
												<div class="c-wysiwyg">
													<h2 id="introduction">Introduction</h2>
<p>Primarily focused on financial gain since its appearance, BlueNoroff (aka. Sapphire Sleet, APT38, Alluring Pisces, Stardust Chollima, and TA444) has adopted new infiltration strategies and malware sets over time, but it still targets blockchain developers, C-level executives, and managers within the Web3/blockchain industry as part of its SnatchCrypto operation. Earlier this year, we conducted research into two malicious campaigns by BlueNoroff under the SnatchCrypto operation, which we dubbed <strong>GhostCall</strong> and <strong>GhostHire</strong>.</p>
<p><strong>GhostCall</strong> heavily targets the macOS devices of executives at tech companies and in the venture capital sector by directly approaching targets via platforms like Telegram, and inviting potential victims to investment-related meetings linked to Zoom-like phishing websites. The victim would join a fake call with genuine recordings of this threat&#8217;s other actual victims rather than deepfakes. The call proceeds smoothly to then encourage the user to update the Zoom client with a script. Eventually, the script downloads ZIP files that result in infection chains deployed on an infected host.</p>
<div id="attachment_117926" style="width: 1610px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071256/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns1.jpg" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117926" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071256/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns1.jpg" alt="GhostCall campaign attack flow" width="1600" height="266" class="size-full wp-image-117926" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071256/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns1.jpg 1600w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071256/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns1-300x50.jpg 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071256/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns1-1024x170.jpg 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071256/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns1-768x128.jpg 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071256/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns1-1536x255.jpg 1536w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071256/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns1-740x123.jpg 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071256/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns1-800x133.jpg 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1600px) 100vw, 1600px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117926" class="wp-caption-text">GhostCall campaign attack flow</p></div>
<p>In the <strong>GhostHire</strong> campaign, BlueNoroff approaches Web3 developers and tricks them into downloading and executing a GitHub repository containing malware under the guise of a skill assessment during a recruitment process. After initial contact and a brief screening, the user is added to a Telegram bot by the recruiter. The bot sends either a ZIP file or a GitHub link, accompanied by a 30-minute time limit to complete the task, while putting pressure on the victim to quickly run the malicious project. Once executed, the project downloads a malicious payload onto the user&#8217;s system. The payload is specifically chosen according to the user agent, which identifies the operating system being used by the victim.</p>
<div id="attachment_117927" style="width: 1442px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071308/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns2.jpg" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117927" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071308/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns2.jpg" alt="GhostHire campaign attack flow" width="1432" height="212" class="size-full wp-image-117927" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071308/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns2.jpg 1432w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071308/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns2-300x44.jpg 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071308/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns2-1024x152.jpg 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071308/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns2-768x114.jpg 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071308/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns2-740x110.jpg 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071308/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns2-800x118.jpg 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1432px) 100vw, 1432px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117927" class="wp-caption-text">GhostHire campaign attack flow</p></div>
<p>We observed the actor utilizing AI in various aspects of their attacks, which enabled them to enhance productivity and meticulously refine their attacks. The infection scheme observed in GhostHire shares structural similarities of infection chains with the GhostCall campaign, and identical malware was detected in both.</p>
<p>We have been tracking these two campaigns since April 2025, particularly observing the continuous emergence of the GhostCall campaign&#8217;s victims on platforms like X. We hope our research will help prevent further damage, and we extend our gratitude to everyone who willingly shared relevant information.</p>
<p><em>The relevant information about GhostCall has already been disclosed by <a href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2024/11/22/microsoft-shares-latest-intelligence-on-north-korean-and-chinese-threat-actors-at-cyberwarcon/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Microsoft</a>, <a href="https://huntability.tech/threat-note-2025-04-23-nk-zoom/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Huntability</a>, <a href="https://www.huntress.com/blog/inside-bluenoroff-web3-intrusion-analysis" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Huntress</a>, <a href="https://fieldeffect.com/blog/zoom-doom-bluenoroff-call-opens-the-door" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Field Effect</a>, and <a href="https://www.sentinelone.com/labs/macos-nimdoor-dprk-threat-actors-target-web3-and-crypto-platforms-with-nim-based-malware" target="_blank" rel="noopener">SentinelOne</a>. However, we cover newly discovered malware chains and provide deeper insights.</em></p>
<h2 id="the-ghostcall-campaign">The GhostCall campaign</h2>
<p>The GhostCall campaign is a sophisticated attack that uses fake online calls with the threat actors posing as fake entrepreneurs or investors to convince targets. GhostCall has been active at least since mid-2023, potentially following the <a href="https://www.jamf.com/blog/bluenoroff-apt-targets-macos-rustbucket-malware/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">RustBucket</a> campaign, which marked BlueNoroff&#8217;s full-scale shift to attacking macOS systems. Windows was the initial focus of the campaign; it soon shifted to macOS to better align with the targets&#8217; predominantly macOS environment, leveraging deceptive video calls to maximize impact.</p>
<p>The GhostCall campaign employs sophisticated fake meeting templates and fake Zoom updaters to deceive targets. Historically, the actor often used excuses related to IP access control, but shifted to audio problems to persuade the target to download the malicious AppleScript code to fix it. Most recently, we observed the actor attempting to transition the target platform from Zoom to Microsoft Teams.</p>
<p>During this investigation, we identified seven distinct multi-component infection chains, a stealer suite, and a keylogger. The modular stealer suite gathers extensive secret files from the host machine, including information about cryptocurrency wallets, Keychain data, package managers, and infrastructure setups. It also captures details related to cloud platforms and DevOps, along with notes, an API key for OpenAI, collaboration application data, and credentials stored within browsers, messengers, and the Telegram messaging app.</p>
<h3 id="initial-access">Initial access</h3>
<p>The actor reaches out to targets on Telegram by impersonating venture capitalists and, in some cases, using <a href="https://x.com/KibahJoseph/status/1899749022404956273" target="_blank" rel="noopener">compromised accounts</a> of real entrepreneurs and startup founders. In their initial messages, the attackers promote investment or partnership opportunities. Once contact is established with the target, <a href="https://slowmist.medium.com/analysis-of-north-korean-hackers-targeted-phishing-scams-on-telegram-872db3f7392b" target="_blank" rel="noopener">they use Calendly</a> to schedule a meeting and then share a meeting link through domains that mimic Zoom. Sometimes, they may send the fake meeting link directly via messages on Telegram. The actor also occasionally uses Telegram&#8217;s hyperlink feature to hide phishing URLs and disguise them as legitimate URLs.</p>
<div id="attachment_117928" style="width: 1480px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071321/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns3.jpg" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117928" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071321/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns3.jpg" alt="Overall behavior of the phishing site" width="1470" height="1030" class="size-full wp-image-117928" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071321/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns3.jpg 1470w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071321/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns3-300x210.jpg 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071321/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns3-1024x717.jpg 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071321/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns3-768x538.jpg 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071321/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns3-500x350.jpg 500w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071321/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns3-740x519.jpg 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071321/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns3-400x280.jpg 400w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071321/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns3-800x561.jpg 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1470px) 100vw, 1470px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117928" class="wp-caption-text">Overall behavior of the phishing site</p></div>
<p>Upon accessing the fake site, the target is presented with a page carefully designed to mirror the appearance of Zoom in a browser. The page uses standard browser features to prompt the user to enable their camera and enter their name. Once activated, the JavaScript logic begins recording and sends a video chunk to the <code>/upload</code> endpoint of the actor&#8217;s fake Zoom domain every second using the POST method.</p>
<div id="attachment_117880" style="width: 850px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034517/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns4.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117880" class="size-full wp-image-117880" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034517/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns4.png" alt="Initial page mimicking Zoom call joining behavior" width="840" height="700" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034517/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns4.png 840w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034517/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns4-300x250.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034517/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns4-768x640.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034517/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns4-420x350.png 420w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034517/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns4-740x617.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034517/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns4-336x280.png 336w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034517/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns4-800x667.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 840px) 100vw, 840px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117880" class="wp-caption-text">Initial page mimicking Zoom call joining behavior</p></div>
<p>Once the target joins, a screen resembling an actual Zoom meeting appears, showing the video feeds of three participants as if they were part of a real session. <a href="https://x.com/jebbery/status/1935663967697027095?s=61" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Based on OSINT</a> we were monitoring, many victims initially believed the videos they encountered were generated by deepfake or AI technology. However, our research revealed that these videos were, in fact, real recordings secretly taken from other victims who had been targeted by the same actor using the same method. Their webcam footage had been unknowingly recorded, then uploaded to attacker-controlled infrastructure, and reused to deceive other victims, making them believe they were participating in a genuine live call. When the video replay ended, the page smoothly transitioned to showing that user&#8217;s profile image, maintaining the illusion of a live call.</p>
<div id="attachment_117881" style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034625/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns5.gif" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117881" class="size-full wp-image-117881" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034625/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns5.gif" alt="Fake Zoom meeting" width="800" height="427" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117881" class="wp-caption-text">Fake Zoom meeting</p></div>
<p>Approximately three to five seconds later, an error message appears below the participants&#8217; feeds, stating that the system is not functioning properly and prompting them to download a Zoom SDK update file through a link labeled &#8220;Update Now&#8221;. However, rather than providing an update, the link downloads a malicious AppleScript file onto macOS and triggers a popup for troubleshooting on Windows.</p>
<div id="attachment_117882" style="width: 1610px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034740/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns6.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117882" class="size-full wp-image-117882" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034740/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns6.png" alt="Clicking the link on macOS (left) and on Windows (right)" width="1600" height="598" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034740/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns6.png 1600w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034740/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns6-300x112.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034740/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns6-1024x383.png 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034740/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns6-768x287.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034740/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns6-1536x574.png 1536w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034740/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns6-936x350.png 936w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034740/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns6-740x277.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034740/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns6-749x280.png 749w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034740/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns6-800x299.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1600px) 100vw, 1600px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117882" class="wp-caption-text">Clicking the link on macOS (left) and on Windows (right)</p></div>
<p>On macOS, clicking the link directly downloads an AppleScript file named <code>Zoom SDK Update.scpt</code> from the actor&#8217;s domain. A small &#8220;Downloads&#8221; coach mark is also displayed, subtly encouraging the user to execute the script by imitating genuine Apple feedback. On Windows, the attack uses <a href="https://www.kaspersky.com/blog/what-is-clickfix/53348/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">the ClickFix technique</a>, where a modal window appears with a seemingly harmless code snippet from a legitimate domain. However, any attempt to copy the code – via the Copy button, right-click and Copy, or Ctrl+C – results in a malicious one-liner being placed in the clipboard instead.</p>
<div id="attachment_117883" style="width: 1493px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034921/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns7.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117883" class="size-full wp-image-117883" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034921/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns7.png" alt="Malicious code upon ClickFix" width="1483" height="555" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034921/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns7.png 1483w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034921/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns7-300x112.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034921/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns7-1024x383.png 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034921/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns7-768x287.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034921/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns7-935x350.png 935w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034921/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns7-740x277.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034921/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns7-748x280.png 748w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27034921/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns7-800x299.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1483px) 100vw, 1483px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117883" class="wp-caption-text">Malicious code upon ClickFix</p></div>
<p>We observed that the actor implemented <a href="https://securelist.com/web-beacons-on-websites-and-in-email/108632/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">beaconing activity</a> within the malicious web page to track victim interactions. The page reports back to their backend infrastructure – likely to assess the success or failure of the targeting. This is accomplished through a series of automatically triggered HTTP GET requests when the victim performs specific actions, as outlined below.</p>
<table>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><strong>Endpoint</strong></td>
<td><strong>Trigger</strong></td>
<td><strong>Purpose</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>/join/{id}/{token}</td>
<td>User clicks Join on the pre-join screen</td>
<td>Track whether the victim entered the meeting</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>/action/{id}/{token}</td>
<td>Update / Troubleshooting SDK modal is shown</td>
<td>Track whether the victim clicked on the update prompt</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>/action1/{id}/{token}</td>
<td>User uses any copy-and-paste method to copy modal window contents</td>
<td>Confirm the clipboard swap likely succeeded</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>/action2/{id}/{token}</td>
<td>User closes modal</td>
<td>Track whether the victim closed the modal</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>In September 2025, we discovered that the group is shifting from cloning the Zoom UI in their attacks to Microsoft Teams. The method of delivering malware remains unchanged. Upon entering the meeting room, a prompt specific to the target&#8217;s operating system appears almost immediately after the background video starts – unlike before. While this is largely similar to Zoom, macOS users also see a separate prompt asking them to download the SDK file.</p>
<div id="attachment_117884" style="width: 1188px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035132/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns8.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117884" class="size-full wp-image-117884" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035132/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns8.png" alt="General fake prompt to update the SDK file (left) and Windows-specific (right)" width="1178" height="612" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035132/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns8.png 1178w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035132/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns8-300x156.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035132/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns8-1024x532.png 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035132/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns8-768x399.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035132/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns8-674x350.png 674w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035132/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns8-740x384.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035132/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns8-539x280.png 539w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035132/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns8-800x416.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1178px) 100vw, 1178px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117884" class="wp-caption-text">General fake prompt to update the SDK file (left) and Windows-specific (right)</p></div>
<p>We were able to obtain the AppleScript (<code>Zoom SDK Update.scpt</code>) the actor claimed was necessary to resolve the issue, which was already widely known through numerous research studies as the entry point for the attack. The script is disguised as an update for the Zoom Meeting SDK and contains nearly 10,000 blank lines that obscure its malicious content. Upon execution, it fetches another AppleScript, which acts as a downloader, from a different fake link using a <code>curl</code> command. There are numerous variants of this &#8220;troubleshooting&#8221; AppleScript, differing in filename, user agent, and contents.</p>
<div id="attachment_117885" style="width: 1234px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns9.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117885" class="size-full wp-image-117885" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns9.png" alt="Snippets of the AppleScript disguised as a Zoom SDK update" width="1224" height="782" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns9.png 1224w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns9-300x192.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns9-1024x654.png 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns9-768x491.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns9-548x350.png 548w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns9-740x473.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns9-438x280.png 438w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27035333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns9-800x511.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1224px) 100vw, 1224px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117885" class="wp-caption-text">Snippets of the AppleScript disguised as a Zoom SDK update</p></div>
<p>If the targeted macOS version is 11 (Monterey) or later, the downloader AppleScript installs a fake application disguised as Zoom or Microsoft Teams into the <code>/private/tmp</code> directory. The application attempts to mimic a legitimate update for Zoom or Teams by displaying a password input popup. Additionally, it downloads a next-stage AppleScript, which we named &#8220;DownTroy&#8221;. This script is expected to check stored passwords and use them to install additional malware with root privileges. We cautiously assess that this would be an evolved version of the older one, disclosed by <a href="https://www.huntress.com/blog/inside-bluenoroff-web3-intrusion-analysis" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Huntress</a>.</p>
<p>Moreover, the downloader script includes a harvesting function that searches for files associated with password management applications (such as Bitwarden, LastPass, 1Password, and Dashlane), the default Notes app (group.com.apple.notes), note-taking apps like Evernote, and the Telegram application installed on the device.</p>
<p>Another notable feature of the downloader script is a bypass of TCC (Transparency, Consent, and Control), a macOS system designed to manage user consent for accessing sensitive resources such as the camera, microphone, AppleEvents/automation, and protected folders like Documents, Downloads, and Desktop. The script works by renaming the user&#8217;s <code>com.apple.TCC</code> directory and then performing offline edits to the <code>TCC.db</code> database. Specifically, it removes any existing entries in the <code>access</code> table related to a client path to be registered in the TCC database and executes <code>INSERT OR REPLACE</code> statements. This process enables the script to grant AppleEvents permissions for automation and file access to a client path controlled by the actor. The script inserts rows for service identifiers used by TCC, including kTCCServiceAppleEvents, <code>kTCCServiceSystemPolicyDocumentsFolder</code>, <code>kTCCServiceSystemPolicyDownloadsFolder</code>, and <code>kTCCServiceSystemPolicyDesktopFolder</code>, and places a hex-encoded code-signature blob (in the <code>csreq</code> style) in the database to meet the requirement for access to be granted. This binary blob must be bound to the target app&#8217;s code signature and evaluated at runtime. Finally, the script attempts to rename the TCC directory back to its original name and calls <code>tccutil reset DeveloperTool</code>.</p>
<p>In the sample we analyzed, the client path is <code>~/Library/Google/Chrome Update</code> – the location the actor uses for their implant. In short, this allows the implant to control other applications, access data from the user&#8217;s Documents, Downloads, and Desktop folders, and execute AppleScripts – all without prompting for user consent.</p>
<div id="attachment_117929" style="width: 1312px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns10.jpg" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117929" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns10.jpg" alt="Initial infection flow" width="1302" height="828" class="size-full wp-image-117929" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns10.jpg 1302w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns10-300x191.jpg 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns10-1024x651.jpg 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns10-768x488.jpg 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns10-550x350.jpg 550w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns10-740x471.jpg 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns10-440x280.jpg 440w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071333/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns10-800x509.jpg 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1302px) 100vw, 1302px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117929" class="wp-caption-text">Initial infection flow</p></div>
<h3 id="multi-stage-execution-chains">Multi-stage execution chains</h3>
<p>According to our telemetry and investigation into the actor&#8217;s infrastructure, DownTroy would download ZIP files that contain various individual infection chains from the actor&#8217;s centralized file hosting server. Although we haven&#8217;t observed how the SysPhon and the SneakMain chain were installed, we suspect they would&#8217;ve been downloaded in the same manner. We have identified not only at least seven multi-stage execution chains retrieved from the server, but also various malware families installed on the infected hosts, including keyloggers and stealers downloaded by CosmicDoor and RooTroy chains.</p>
<table>
<tbody>
<tr>

<p>The actor has been introducing new malware chains by adapting new programming languages and developing new components since 2023. Before that, they employed standalone malware families, but later evolved into a modular structure consisting of launchers, injectors, installers, loaders, and droppers. This modular approach enables the malicious behavior to be divided into smaller components, making it easier to bypass security products and evade detection. Most of the final payloads in these chains have the capability to download additional AppleScript files or execute commands to retrieve subsequent-stage payloads.</p>
<p>Interestingly, the actor initially favored Rust for writing malware but ultimately switched to the Nim language. Meanwhile, other programming languages like C++, Python, Go, and Swift have also been utilized. The C++ language was employed to develop the injector malware as well as the base application within the injector, but the application was later rewritten in Swift. Go was also used to develop certain components of the malware chain, such as the installer and dropper, but these were later switched to Nim as well.</p>
<h3 id="zoomclutch-teamsclutch-the-fake-zoom-teams-application">ZoomClutch/TeamsClutch: the fake Zoom/Teams application</h3>
<p>During our research of a macOS intrusion on a victim&#8217;s machine, we found a suspicious application resembling a Zoom client executing from an atypical, writable path – <code>/tmp/zoom.app/Contents/MacOS</code> – rather than the standard <code>/Applications</code> directory. Analysis showed that the binary was not an official Zoom build but a custom implant compiled on macOS 14.5 (24F74) with <a href="https://developer.apple.com/xcode/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Xcode</a> 16 beta 2 (16C5032a) against the macOS 15.2 SDK. The app is ad‑hoc signed, and its bundle identifier is hard‑coded to <code>us.zoom.com</code> to mimic the legitimate client.</p>
<p>The implant is written in Swift and functions as a macOS credentials harvester, disguised as the Zoom videoconferencing application. It features a well-developed user interface using Swift&#8217;s modern UI frameworks that closely mimics the Zoom application icon, Apple password prompts, and other authentic elements.</p>
<div id="attachment_117887" style="width: 969px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040419/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns11.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117887" class="size-full wp-image-117887" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040419/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns11.png" alt="ZoomClutch prompting the victim to enter their password" width="959" height="474" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040419/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns11.png 959w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040419/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns11-300x148.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040419/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns11-768x380.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040419/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns11-708x350.png 708w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040419/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns11-740x366.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040419/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns11-566x280.png 566w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040419/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns11-800x395.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 959px) 100vw, 959px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117887" class="wp-caption-text">ZoomClutch prompting the victim to enter their password</p></div>
<p>ZoomClutch steals macOS passwords by displaying a fake Zoom dialog, then sends the captured credentials to the C2 server. However, before exfiltrating the data, ZoomClutch first validates the credentials locally using Apple&#8217;s <a href="https://developer.apple.com/documentation/opendirectory" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Open Directory (OD)</a> to filter out typos and incorrect entries, mirroring macOS&#8217;s own authentication flow. OD manages accounts and authentication processes for both local and external directories. Local user data sits at <code>/var/db/dslocal/nodes/Default/users/</code> as plists with <code>PBKDF2‑SHA512</code>  hashes. The malware creates an <code>ODSession</code>, then opens a local <code>ODNode</code> via <code>kODNodeTypeLocalNodes (0x2200/8704)</code> to scope operations to <code>/Local/Default</code>.</p>
<p>It subsequently calls <code>verifyPassword:error:</code> to check the password, which re-hashes the input password using the stored salt and iterations, returning <code>true</code> if there is a match. If verification fails, ZoomClutch re-prompts the user and shortly displays a &#8220;wrong password&#8221; popup with a shake animation. On success, it hides the dialog, displays a &#8220;Zoom Meeting SDK has been updated successfully&#8221; message, and the validated credentials are covertly sent to the C2 server.</p>
<div id="attachment_117888" style="width: 1067px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040818/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns12.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117888" class="size-full wp-image-117888" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040818/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns12.png" alt="ZoomClutch success window displayed after password validation" width="1057" height="541" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040818/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns12.png 1057w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040818/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns12-300x154.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040818/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns12-1024x524.png 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040818/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns12-768x393.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040818/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns12-684x350.png 684w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040818/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns12-740x379.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040818/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns12-547x280.png 547w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040818/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns12-800x409.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1057px) 100vw, 1057px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117888" class="wp-caption-text">ZoomClutch success window displayed after password validation</p></div>
<p>All passwords entered in the prompt are logged to <code>~/Library/Logs/keybagd_events.log</code>. The malware then creates a file at <code>~/Library/Logs/&lt;username&gt;_auth.log</code> to store the verified password in plain text. This file is subsequently uploaded to a C2 URL using <code>curl</code>.</p>
<p><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040937/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns13.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="aligncenter size-full wp-image-117889" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040937/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns13.png" alt="" width="935" height="132" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040937/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns13.png 935w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040937/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns13-300x42.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040937/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns13-768x108.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040937/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns13-740x104.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27040937/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns13-800x113.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 935px) 100vw, 935px" /></a></p>
<p>With medium-high confidence, we assess that the malware was part of BlueNoroff&#8217;s workflow needed to initiate the execution flow outlined in the subsequent infection chains.</p>
<p>The TeamsClutch malware that mimics a legitimate Microsoft Teams functions similarly to ZoomClutch, but with its logo and some text elements replaced.</p>
<div id="attachment_117890" style="width: 1610px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27041026/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns14.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117890" class="size-full wp-image-117890" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27041026/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns14.png" alt="TeamsClutch authentication and success windows" width="1600" height="595" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27041026/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns14.png 1600w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27041026/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns14-300x112.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27041026/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns14-1024x381.png 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27041026/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns14-768x286.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27041026/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns14-1536x571.png 1536w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27041026/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns14-941x350.png 941w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27041026/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns14-740x275.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27041026/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns14-753x280.png 753w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27041026/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns14-800x298.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1600px) 100vw, 1600px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117890" class="wp-caption-text">TeamsClutch authentication and success windows</p></div>
<h3 id="downtroy-v1-chain">DownTroy v1 chain</h3>
<p>The DownTroy v1 chain consists of a launcher and a dropper, which ultimately loads the DownTroy.macOS malware written in AppleScript.</p>
<ul>
<li>Dropper: a dropper file named <code>"trustd"</code>, written in Go</li>
<li>Launcher: a launcher file named <code>"watchdog"</code>, written in Go</li>
<li>Final payload: DownTroy.macOS written in AppleScript</li>
</ul>
<p>The dropper operates in two distinct modes: initialization and operational. When the binary is executed with a machine ID (<code>mid</code>) as the sole argument, it enters initialization mode and updates the configuration file located at <code>~/Library/Assistant/CustomVocabulary/com.applet.safari/local_log</code> using the provided mid and encrypts it with RC4. It then runs itself without any arguments to transition into operational mode. In case the binary is launched without any arguments, it enters operational mode directly. In this mode, it retrieves the previously saved configuration and uses the RC4 key <code>NvZGluZz0iVVRGLTgiPz4KPCF</code> to decrypt it. It is important to note that the <code>mid</code> value must first be included in the configuration during initialization mode, as it is essential for subsequent actions.</p>
<p>It then decodes a hard-coded, base64-encoded string associated with DownTroy.macOS. This AppleScript contains a placeholder value, <code>%mail_id%</code>, which is replaced with the initialized <code>mid</code> value from the configuration. The modified script is saved to a temporary file named <code>local.lock</code> within the <code>&lt;BasePath&gt;</code> directory from the configuration, with 0644 permissions applied, meaning that only the script owner can modify it. The malware then uses <code>osascript</code> to execute DownTroy.macOS and sets <code>Setpgid=1</code> to isolate the process group. DownTroy.macOS is responsible for downloading additional scripts from its C2 server until the system is rebooted.</p>
<p>The dropper implements a signal handling procedure to monitor for termination attempts. Initially, it reads the entire <code>trustd</code> (itself) and <code>watchdog</code> binary files into memory, storing them in a buffer before deleting the original files. Upon receiving a SIGINT or SIGTERM signal indicating that the process should terminate, the recovery mechanism activates to maintain persistence. While SIGINT is a signal used to interrupt a running process by the user from the terminal using the keyboard shortcut Ctrl + C, SIGTERM is a signal that requests a process to terminate gracefully.</p>
<p>The recovery mechanism begins by recreating the <code>&lt;BasePath&gt;</code> directory with intentionally insecure <code>0777</code> permissions (meaning that all users have the read, write, and execute permissions). Next, it writes both binaries back to disk from memory, assigning them executable permissions (<code>0755</code>), and also creates a plist file to ensure the automatic restart of this process chain.</p>
<ul>
<li>trustd: <code>trustd</code> in the <code>&lt;BasePath&gt;</code> directory</li>
<li>watchdog: <code>~/Library/Assistant/SafariUpdate</code> and <code>watchdog</code> in the <code>&lt;BasePath&gt;</code> directory</li>
<li>plist: <code>~/Library/LaunchAgents/com.applet.safari.plist</code></li>
</ul>
<p>The contents of the plist file are hard-coded into the dropper in base64-encoded form. When decoded, the template represents a standard macOS LaunchAgent plist containing the placeholder tokens <code>#path</code> and <code>#label</code>. The malware replaces these tokens to customize the template. The final plist configuration ensures the launcher automatic execution by setting <code>RunAtLoad</code> to true (starts at login), <code>KeepAlive</code> to true (restarts if terminated), and <code>LaunchOnlyOnce</code> to true.</p>
<ul>
<li><code>#path</code> is replaced with the path to the copied <code>watchdog</code></li>
<li><code>#label</code> is replaced with <code>com.applet.safari</code> to masquerade as a legitimate Safari-related component</li>
</ul>
<p><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27042035/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns15.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="aligncenter size-full wp-image-117891" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27042035/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns15.png" alt="" width="958" height="724" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27042035/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns15.png 958w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27042035/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns15-300x227.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27042035/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns15-768x580.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27042035/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns15-200x150.png 200w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27042035/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns15-463x350.png 463w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27042035/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns15-740x559.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27042035/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns15-370x280.png 370w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27042035/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns15-800x605.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 958px) 100vw, 958px" /></a></p>
<p>The main feature of the discovered launcher is its ability to load the same configuration file located at <code>~/Library/Assistant/CustomVocabulary/com.applet.safari/local_log</code>. It reads the file and uses the RC4 algorithm to decrypt its contents with the same hard-coded 25-byte key: <code>NvZGluZz0iVVRGLTgiPz4KPCF</code>. After decryption, the loader extracts the <code>&lt;BasePath&gt;</code> value from the JSON object, which specifies the location of the next payload. It then executes a file named <code>trustd</code> from this path, disguising it as a legitimate macOS system process.</p>
<p>We identified another version of the loader, distinguished by the configuration path that contains the <code>&lt;BasePath&gt;</code> – this time, the configuration file was located at <code>/Library/Graphics/com.applet.safari/local_log</code>. The second version is used when the actor has gained root-level permissions, likely achieved through ZoomClutch during the initial infection.</p>
<h3 id="cosmicdoor-chain">CosmicDoor chain</h3>
<p>The CosmicDoor chain begins with an injector malware that we have named &#8220;GillyInjector&#8221; written in C++, which was also described by <a href="https://www.huntress.com/blog/inside-bluenoroff-web3-intrusion-analysis" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Huntress</a> and <a href="https://www.sentinelone.com/labs/macos-nimdoor-dprk-threat-actors-target-web3-and-crypto-platforms-with-nim-based-malware/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">SentinelOne</a>. This malware includes an encrypted baseApp and an encrypted malicious payload.</p>
<ul>
<li>Injector: GillyInjector written in C++</li>
<li>BaseApp: a benign application written in C++ or Swift</li>
<li>Final payload: CosmicDoor.macOS written in Nim</li>
</ul>
<p>The <code>syscon.zip</code> file downloaded from the file hosting server contains the &#8220;a&#8221; binary that has been identified as GillyInjector designed to run a benign Mach-O app and inject a malicious payload into it at runtime. Both the injector and the benign application are ad-hoc signed, similar to ZoomClutch. GillyInjector employs a technique known as Task Injection, a rare and sophisticated method observed on macOS systems.</p>
<p>The injector operates in two modes: wiper mode and injector mode. When executed with the <code>--d</code> flag, GillyInjector activates its destructive capabilities. It begins by enumerating all files in the current directory and securely deleting each one. Once all files in the directory are unrecoverably wiped, GillyInjector proceeds to remove the directory itself. When executed with a filename and password, GillyInjector operates as a process injector. It creates a benign application with the given filename in the current directory and uses the provided password to derive an AES decryption key.</p>
<p>The benign Mach-O application and its embedded payload are encrypted with a customized AES-256 algorithm in ECB mode (although similar to the structure of the OFB mode) and then base64-encoded. To decrypt, the first 16 bytes of the encoded string are extracted as the salt for a PBKDF2 key derivation process. This process uses 10,000 iterations, and a user-provided password to generate a SHA-256-based key. The derived key is then used to decrypt the base64-decoded ciphertext that follows.</p>
<div id="attachment_117930" style="width: 1610px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071345/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns16.jpg" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117930" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071345/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns16.jpg" alt="Base application and payload decryption" width="1600" height="369" class="size-full wp-image-117930" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071345/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns16.jpg 1600w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071345/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns16-300x69.jpg 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071345/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns16-1024x236.jpg 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071345/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns16-768x177.jpg 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071345/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns16-1536x354.jpg 1536w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071345/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns16-1518x350.jpg 1518w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071345/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns16-740x171.jpg 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071345/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns16-1214x280.jpg 1214w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071345/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns16-800x185.jpg 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1600px) 100vw, 1600px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117930" class="wp-caption-text">Base application and payload decryption</p></div>
<p>The ultimately injected payload is identified as CosmicDoor.macOS, written in Nim. The main feature of CosmicDoor is that it communicates with the C2 server using the WSS protocol, and it provides remote control functionality such as receiving and executing commands.</p>
<p>Our telemetry indicates that at least three versions of CosmicDoor.macOS have been detected so far, each written in different cross-platform programming languages, including <a href="https://g-les.github.io/yara/2024/01/04/100DaysofYARA-CosmicRust.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Rust</a>, Python, and Nim. We also discovered that the Windows variant of CosmicDoor was developed in Go, demonstrating that the threat actor has actively used this malware across both Windows and macOS environments since 2023. Based on our investigation, the development of CosmicDoor likely followed this order: CosmicDoor.Windows in Go → CosmicDoor.macOS in Rust → CosmicDoor in Python → CosmicDoor.macOS in Nim. The Nim version, the most recently identified, stands out from the others primarily due to its updated execution chain, including the use of GillyInjector.</p>
<p>Except for the appearance of the injector, the differences between the Windows version and other versions are not significant. On Windows, the fourth to sixth characters of all RC4 key values are initialized to <code>123</code>. In addition, the CosmicDoor.macOS version, written in Nim, has an updated value for <code>COMMAND_KEY</code>.</p>

<h3 id="silentsiphon-a-stealer-suite-for-harvesting">SilentSiphon: a stealer suite for harvesting</h3>
<p>During our investigation, we discovered that CosmicDoor downloads a stealer suite composed of various bash scripts, which we dubbed &#8220;SilentSiphon&#8221;. In most observed infections, multiple bash shell scripts were created on infected hosts shortly after the installation of CosmicDoor. These scripts were used to collect and exfiltrate data to the actor&#8217;s C2 servers.</p>
<p>The file named <code>upl.sh</code> functions as an orchestration launcher, which aggregates multiple standalone data-extraction modules identified on the victim&#8217;s system.</p><!-- Urvanov Syntax Highlighter v2.9.0 -->

		<div id="urvanov-syntax-highlighter-690378cfa31b6574480885" class="urvanov-syntax-highlighter-syntax crayon-theme-classic urvanov-syntax-highlighter-font-monaco urvanov-syntax-highlighter-os-pc print-yes notranslate" data-settings=" minimize scroll-mouseover" style=" margin-top: 12px; margin-bottom: 12px; font-size: 12px !important; line-height: 15px !important;">

			</div>
		</div>
<!-- [Format Time: 0.0001 seconds] -->
<p>
The launcher first uses the command who <code>| tail -n1 | awk '{print $1}'</code> to identify the username of the currently logged-in macOS user, thus ensuring that all subsequent file paths are resolved within the ongoing active session – regardless of whether the script is executed by another account or via Launch Agents. However, both the hard-coded C2 server and the username can be modified with the <code>-h</code> and <code>-u</code> flags, a feature consistent with other modules analyzed in this research. The orchestrator executes five embedded modules located in the same directory, removing each immediately after it completes exfiltration.</p>
<p>The stealer suite harvests data from the compromised host as follows:</p>
<ol>
<li>upl.sh is the orchestrator and Apple Notes stealer.<br />
It targets Apple Notes at /private/var/tmp/group.com.apple.notes.<br />
It stores the data at /private/var/tmp/notes_&lt;username&gt;.</li>
<li>cpl.sh is the browser extension stealer module.<br />
It targets:</li>
<ul>
<li><strong>Local storage for extensions:</strong> the entire &#8220;Local Extension Settings&#8221; directory of Chromium-based web browsers, such as Chrome, Brave, Arc, Edge, and Ecosia</li>
<li><strong>Browser&#8217;s built-in database:</strong> directories corresponding to Exodus Web3 Wallet, Coinbase Wallet extension, Crypto.com Onchain Extension, Manta Wallet, 1Password, and Sui wallet in the &#8220;IndexedDB&#8221; directory</li>
<li><strong>Extension list:</strong> the list of installed extensions in the &#8220;Extensions&#8221; directory<br />
Stores the data at /private/var/tmp/cpl_&lt;username&gt;/&lt;browser&gt;/*</li>
</ul>
<li>ubd.sh is the browser credentials and macOS Keychains stealer module.<br />
It targets:</li>
<ul>
<li><strong>Credentials stored in the browsers:</strong> Local State, History, Cookies, Sessions, Web Data, Bookmarks, Login Data, Session Storage, Local Storage, and IndexedDB directories of Chromium-based web browsers, such as Chrome, Brave, Arc, Edge, and Ecosia</li>
<li><strong>Credentials in the Keychain:</strong> /Library/Keychains/System.keychain and ~/Library/Keychains/login.keychain-db<br />
It stores the data at /private/var/tmp/ubd_&lt;username&gt;/*</li>
</ul>
<li>secrets.sh is the secrets stealer module.<br />
It targets:</li>
<ul>
<li><strong>Version Control:</strong> GitHub (.config/gh), GitLab (.config/glab-cli), and Bitbucket (.bit/config)</li>
<li><strong>Package manager:</strong> npm (.npmrc), Yarn (.yarnrc.yml), Python pip (.pypirc), RubyGems (.gem/credentials), Rust cargo (.cargo/credentials), and .NET Nuget (.nuget/NuGet.Config)</li>
<li><strong>Cloud/Infrastructure:</strong> AWS (.aws), Google Cloud (.config/gcloud), Azure (.azure), Oracle Cloud (.oci), Akamai Linode (.config/linode-cli), and DigitalOcean API (.config/doctl/config.yaml)</li>
<li><strong>Cloud Application Platform:</strong> Vercel (.vercel), Cloudflare (.wrangler/config), Netlify (.netfily), Stripe (.config/stripe/config.toml), Firebase (.config/configstore/firebase-tools.json), Twilio (.twilio-cli)</li>
<li><strong>DevOps/IaC:</strong> CircleCI (.circleci/cli.yml), Pulumi (.pulumi/credentials.json), and HashiCorp (.vault-token)</li>
<li><strong>Security/Authentication:</strong> SSH (.ssh) and FTP/cURL/Wget (.netrc)</li>
<li><strong>Blockchain Related:</strong> Sui Blockchain (.sui), Solana (.config/solana), NEAR Blockchain (.near-credentials), Aptos Blockchain (.aptos), and Algorand (.algorand)</li>
<li><strong>Container Related</strong>: Docker (.docker) and Kubernetes (.kube)</li>
<li><strong>AI:</strong> OpenAI (.openai)<br />
It stores the data at /private/var/tmp/secrets_backup_&lt;current time&gt;/&lt;username&gt;/*</li>
</ul>
<li>uad.sh is the password‑vault stealer module<br />
It targets:</li>
<ul>
<li><strong>Password manager:</strong> 1Password 8, 1Password 7, Bitwarden, LastPass, and Dashlane</li>
<li><strong>Note-taking:</strong> Evernote and Notion</li>
<li><strong>Collaboration suites:</strong> Slack</li>
<li><strong>Messenger:</strong> Skype (inactive), WeChat (inactive), and WhatsApp (inactive)</li>
<li><strong>Cryptocurrency:</strong> Ledger Live, Hiro StacksWallet, Tonkeeper, MyTonWallet, and MetaMask (inactive)</li>
<li><strong>Remote Monitoring and Management:</strong> AnyDesk<br />
It stores the data at /private/var/tmp/&lt;username&gt;_&lt;target application&gt;_&lt;current time&gt;/*</li>
</ul>
<li>utd.sh is the Telegram stealer module<br />
It targets:</li>
<ul>
<li>On macOS version 14 and later:
<ul>
<li>Telegram&#8217;s cached resources, such as chat history and media files</li>
<li>Encrypted geolocation cache</li>
<li>AES session keys used for account takeover</li>
<li>Legacy sandbox cache</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>On macOS versions earlier than 14:
<ul>
<li>List of configured Telegram accounts</li>
<li>Export-key vault</li>
<li>Full chat DB, messages, contacts, files, and cached media<br />
It stores the data at /private/var/tmp/Telegrams_&lt;username&gt;/*</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
</ol>
<p>These extremely extensive targets allow the actor to expand beyond simple credentials to encompass their victims&#8217; entire infrastructure. This includes Telegram accounts exploitable for further attacks, supply chain configuration details, and collaboration tools revealing personal notes and business interactions with other users. Notably, the attackers even target the .openai folder to secretly use ChatGPT with the user&#8217;s account.</p>
<p>The collected information is immediately archived with the <code>ditto -ck</code> command and uploaded to the initialized C2 server via <code">curl</code> command, using the same approach as in ZoomClutch.</p>
<p><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27051922/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns17.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="aligncenter size-full wp-image-117894" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27051922/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns17.png" alt="" width="935" height="137" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27051922/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns17.png 935w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27051922/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns17-300x44.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27051922/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns17-768x113.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27051922/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns17-740x108.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27051922/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns17-800x117.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 935px) 100vw, 935px" /></a></p>
<h3 id="rootroy-chain">RooTroy chain</h3>
<p>We identified a ZIP archive downloaded from the file hosting server that contains a three-component toolset. The final payload, RooTroy.macOS, was also documented in the Huntress&#8217;s <a href="https://www.huntress.com/blog/inside-bluenoroff-web3-intrusion-analysis" target="_blank" rel="noopener">blog</a>, but we were able to obtain its full chain. The archive includes the following:</p>
<ul>
<li>Installer: the primary installer file named <code>"rtv4inst"</code>, written in Go</li>
<li>Loader: an auxiliary loader file named <code>"st"</code> and identified as the Nimcore loader, written in Nim</li>
<li>Injector: an injector file named <code>"wt"</code>, which is identified as GillyInjector, written in C++</li>
<li>Final payload: RooTroy.macOS, written in Go</li>
</ul>
<p>Upon the execution of the installer, it immediately checks for the presence of other components and terminates if any are missing. Additionally, it verifies that it has accepted at least two command-line arguments to function properly, as follows.</p><!-- Urvanov Syntax Highlighter v2.9.0 -->

		<div id="urvanov-syntax-highlighter-690378cfa31c7502991479" class="urvanov-syntax-highlighter-syntax crayon-theme-classic urvanov-syntax-highlighter-font-monaco urvanov-syntax-highlighter-os-pc print-yes notranslate" data-settings=" minimize scroll-mouseover" style=" margin-top: 12px; margin-bottom: 12px; font-size: 12px !important; line-height: 15px !important;">

rvt4inst &lt;MID&gt; &lt;C2&gt; [&lt;Additional C2 domains…&gt;]</textarea></div>
