				</td>
						<td class="urvanov-syntax-highlighter-code"><div class="crayon-pre" style="font-size: 12px !important; line-height: 15px !important; -moz-tab-size:4; -o-tab-size:4; -webkit-tab-size:4; tab-size:4;"><div class="crayon-line" id="urvanov-syntax-highlighter-690378cfa31c7502991479-1"><span class="crayon-v">rvt4inst</span><span class="crayon-h"> </span><span class="crayon-o">&lt;</span><span class="crayon-v">MID</span><span class="crayon-o">&gt;</span><span class="crayon-h"> </span><span class="crayon-o">&lt;</span><span class="crayon-v">C2</span><span class="crayon-o">&gt;</span><span class="crayon-h"> </span><span class="crayon-sy">[</span><span class="crayon-o">&lt;</span><span class="crayon-e">Additional </span><span class="crayon-e">C2 </span><span class="crayon-i">domains</span>…<span class="crayon-o">&gt;</span><span class="crayon-sy">]</span></div></div></td>
					</tr>
				</table>
			</div>
		</div>
<!-- [Format Time: 0.0001 seconds] -->
<p>
<ul>
<li>MID (Machine ID): unique identifier for victim tracking</li>
<li>C2: primary command‑and‑control domain</li>
<li>Additional C2 values can be supplied</li>
</ul>
<p>On the first launch, the installer creates several directories and files that imitate legitimate macOS components. Note that these paths are abused only for camouflage; none are genuine system locations.</p>
<table>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><strong>Num</strong></td>
<td><strong>Path</strong></td>
<td><strong>Role</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>1</td>
<td>/Library/Google/Cache/.cfg</td>
<td>Configuration</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>2</td>
<td>/Library/Application Support/Logitechs/versions</td>
<td>Not identified</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>3</td>
<td>/Library/Application Support/Logitechs/bin/Update Check</td>
<td>Final location of the Nimcore loader (st)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>4</td>
<td>/Library/Storage/Disk</td>
<td>baseApp&#8217;s potential location 1</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>5</td>
<td>/Library/Storage/Memory</td>
<td>baseApp&#8217;s potential location 2</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>6</td>
<td>/Library/Storage/CPU/cpumons</td>
<td>Final location of GillyInjector (wt)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>7</td>
<td>/Library/LaunchDaemons/&lt;bundle ID&gt;.plist</td>
<td>.plist path for launching st</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>8</td>
<td>/private/var/tmp/.lesshst</td>
<td>Contains the .plist path</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The installer uses the hard‑coded key <code>3DD226D0B700F33974F409142DEFB62A8CD172AE5F2EB9BEB7F5750EB1702E2A</code> to serialize its runtime parameters into an RC4‑encrypted blob. The resulting encrypted value is written as <code>.cfg</code> inside <code>/Library/Google/Cache/</code>.</p>
<p>The installer then implements a naming mechanism for the plist name through dynamic bundle ID generation, where it scans legitimate applications in <code>/Applications</code> to create convincing identifiers. It enumerates <code>.app</code> bundles, extracts their names, and combines them with service-oriented terms like &#8220;agent&#8221;, &#8220;webhelper&#8221;, &#8220;update&#8221;, &#8220;updater&#8221;, &#8220;startup&#8221;, &#8220;service&#8221;, &#8220;cloudd&#8221;, &#8220;daemon&#8221;, &#8220;keystone.agent&#8221;, &#8220;update.agent&#8221;, or &#8220;installer&#8221; to construct bundle IDs, such as &#8220;com.safari.update&#8221; or &#8220;com.chrome.service&#8221;. If the bundle ID generation process fails for any reason, the malware defaults to &#8220;com.apple.updatecheck&#8221; as a hard-coded fallback identifier.</p>
<p>The installer then deploys the auxiliary binaries from the ZIP extraction directory to their final system locations. The Nimcore loader (<code>st</code>) is copied to <code>/Library/Application Support/Logitechs/bin/Update Check</code>. The GillyInjector binary is renamed to <code>cpumons</code> in the <code>/Library/Storage/CPU</code> path. Both files receive <code>0755</code> permissions to ensure executability.</p>
<p>Later, a persistence mechanism is implemented through macOS Launch Daemon plists. The plist template contains four placeholder fields that are filled in during generation:</p>
<ul>
<li>The <code>Label</code> field receives the dynamically generated bundle ID.</li>
<li>The <code>SERVER_AUTH_KEY</code> environment variable is populated with the GillyInjector&#8217;s path <code>/Library/Storage/CPU/cpumons</code> that is RC4-encrypted using the hard-coded key <code>"yniERNUgGUHuAhgCzMAi"</code> and then base64-encoded.</li>
<li>The <code>CLIENT_AUTH_KEY</code> environment variable receives the hard-coded value <code>".."</code>.</li>
<li>The <code>Program</code> field points to the installed Nimcore loader&#8217;s path.</li>
</ul>
<p><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27052736/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns18.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="aligncenter size-full wp-image-117895" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27052736/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns18.png" alt="" width="960" height="814" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27052736/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns18.png 960w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27052736/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns18-300x254.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27052736/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns18-768x651.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27052736/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns18-413x350.png 413w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27052736/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns18-740x627.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27052736/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns18-330x280.png 330w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27052736/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns18-800x678.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 960px) 100vw, 960px" /></a></p>
<p>The installer completes the persistence setup by using legitimate <code>launchctl</code> commands to activate the persistence mechanism, ensuring the Nimcore loader is executed. It first runs &#8220;<code>launchctl unload &lt;bundle ID&gt;.plist</code>&#8221; on any existing plist with the same name to remove previous instances, then executes &#8220;<code>launchctl load &lt;bundle ID&gt;.plist</code>&#8221; to activate the new persistence configuration through <code>/bin/zsh -c</code>.</p>
<p>The second stage in this execution chain is the Nimcore loader, which is deployed by the installer and specified in the <code>Program</code> field of the plist file. This loader reads the <code>SERVER_AUTH_KEY</code> environment variable with <code>getenv()</code>, base64-decodes the value, and decrypts it with the same RC4 key used by the installer. The loader is able to retrieve the necessary value because both <code>SERVER_AUTH_KEY</code> and <code>CLIENT_AUTH_KEY</code> are provided in the plist file and filled in by the installer. After decryption, it invokes <code>posix_spawn()</code> to launch GillyInjector.</p>
<p>GillyInjector is the third component in the RooTroy chain and follows the same behavior as described in the CosmicDoor chain. In this instance, however, the password used for generation is hard-coded as <code>xy@bomb#</code> within the component. The baseApp is primarily responsible for displaying only a simple message and acts as a carrier to keep the injected final payload in memory during runtime.</p>
<p>The final payload is identified as RooTroy.macOS, written in Go. Upon initialization, RooTroy.macOS reads its configuration from <code>/Library/Google/Cache/.cfg</code>, a file created by the primary installer, and uses the RC4 algorithm with the same <code>3DD226D0B700F33974F409142DEFB62A8CD172AE5F2EB9BEB7F5750EB1702E2A</code> key to decrypt it. If it fails to read the config file, it removes all files at <code>/Library/Google/Cache</code> and exits.</p>
<p>As the payload is executed at every boot time via a plist setup, it prevents duplicate execution by checking the .pid file in the same directory. If a process ID is found in the file, it terminates the corresponding process and writes the current process ID into the file. Additionally, it writes the string <code>{"rt": "4.0.0."}</code> into the <code>.version</code> file, also located in the same directory, to indicate the current version. This string is encrypted using RC4 with the key <code>C4DB903322D17C8CBF1D1DB55124854C0B070D6ECE54162B6A4D06DF24C572DF</code>.</p>
<p>This backdoor executes commands from the <code>/Library/Google/Cache/.startup</code> file line by line. Each line is executed via <code>/bin/zsh -c "[command]"</code> in a separate process. It also monitors the user&#8217;s login status and re-executes the commands when the user logs back in after being logged out.</p>
<p>Next, RooTroy collects and lists all mounted volumes and running processes. It then enters an infinite loop, repeatedly re-enumerating the volumes to detect any changes – such as newly connected USB drives, network shares, or unmounted devices – and uses a different function to identify changes in the list of processes since the last iteration. It sends the collected information to the C2 server via a POST request to <code>/update</code> endpoint with <code>Content-Type: application/json</code>.</p>
<p>The <code>data</code> field in the response from the C2 server is executed directly via AppleScript with <code>osascript -e</code>. When both the <code>url</code> and <code>auth</code> fields are present, RooTroy connects to the URL with <code>GET</code> method and the <code>Authorization</code> header to retrieve additional files. Then it sleeps for five seconds and repeats the process.</p>
<p>Additional files are loaded as outlined below:</p>
<ol>
<li>Generate a random 10-character file name in the temp directory: <code>/private/tmp/[random-chars]{10}.zip</code>.</li>
<li>Save the downloaded data to that file path.</li>
<li>Extract the ZIP file using <code>ditto -xk /private/tmp/[random-chars]{10}.zip /private/tmp/[random-chars]{10}</code>.</li>
<li>Make the file executable using <code>chmod +x /private/tmp/[random-chars]{10}/install</code>.</li>
<li>Likely install additional components by executing <code>/bin/zsh /private/tmp/[random-chars]{10}/install /private/tmp/[random-chars]{10} /private/tmp/[random-chars]{10}/.result</code>.</li>
<li>Check the <code>.result</code> file for the string &#8220;success&#8221;.</li>
<li>Send result to <code>/report</code> endpoint.</li>
<li>Increment the <code>cid</code> field and save the configuration.</li>
<li>Clean up all temp files.</li>
</ol>
<p>We also observed the RooTroy backdoor deploying files named <code>keyboardd</code> to the <code>/Library/keyboard</code> directory and airmond to the <code>/Library/airplay</code> path, which were confirmed to be a keylogger and an infostealer.</p>
<h3 id="realtimetroy-chain">RealTimeTroy chain</h3>
<p>We recently discovered GillyInjector containing an encrypted RealTimeTroy.macOS payload from the public multiscanning service.</p>
<ul>
<li>Injector: GillyInjector written in C++</li>
<li>baseApp: the file named &#8220;ChromeUpdates&#8221; in the same ZIP file (not secured)</li>
<li>Final payload: RealTimeTroy.macOS, written in Go</li>
</ul>
<p>RealTimeTroy is a straightforward backdoor written in the Go programming language that communicates with a C2 server using the WSS protocol. We have secured both versions of this malware. In the second version, the baseApp named &#8220;ChromeUpdates&#8221; should be bundled along with the injector into a ZIP file. While the baseApp data is included in the same manner as in other GillyInjector instances, it is not actually used. Instead, the <code>ChromeUpdates</code> file is copied to the path specified as the first parameter and executed as the base application for the injection.</p>
<p>This will be explained in more detail in the GhostHire campaign section as the payload RealTimeTroy.macOS performs actions identical to the Windows version, with some differences in the commands. Like the Windows version, it injects the payload upon receiving command 16. However, it uses functionality similar to GillyInjector to inject the payload received from the C2. The password for AES decryption and the hardcoded baseApp within RealTimeTroy have been identified as being identical to the ones contained within the existing GillyInjector (<code>MD5 76ACE3A6892C25512B17ED42AC2EBD05</code>).</p>
<p>Additionally, two new commands have been added compared to the Windows version, specifically for handling commands via the pseudo-terminal. Commands <code>20</code> and <code>21</code> are used to respectively spawn and exit the terminal, which is used for executing commands received from command <code>8</code>.</p>
<p>We found the <code>vcs.time</code> metadata within the second version of RealTimeTroy.macOS, which implies the commit time of this malware, and this value was set to <code>2025-05-29T12:22:09Z</code>.</p>
<h3 id="sneakmain-chain">SneakMain chain</h3>
<p>During our investigation into various incidents, we were able to identify another infection chain involving the macOS version of SneakMain in the victims&#8217; infrastructures. Although we were not able to secure the installer malware, it would operate similar to the RooTroy chain, considering the behavior of its loader.</p>
<ul>
<li>Installer: the primary installer (not secured)</li>
<li>Loader: Identified as Nimcore loader, written in Nim</li>
<li>Final payload: SneakMain.macOS, written in Nim</li>
</ul>
<p>The Nimcore loader reads the <code>SERVER_AUTH_KEY</code> and <code>CLIENT_AUTH_KEY</code> environment variables upon execution. Given the flow of the RooTroy chain, we can assume that these values are provided through the plist file installed by an installer component. Next, the values are base64-decoded and then decrypted using the RC4 algorithm with the hard-coded key <code>vnoknknklfewRFRewfjkdlIJDKJDF</code>, which is consistently used throughout the SneakMain chain. The decrypted <code>SERVER_AUTH_KEY</code> value should represent the path to the next payload to be executed by the loader, while the decrypted <code>CLIENT_AUTH_KEY</code> value is saved to the configuration file located at <code>/private/var/tmp/cfg</code>.</p>
<p>We have observed that this loader was installed under the largest number of various names among malware as follows:</p>
<ul>
<li>/Library/Application Support/frameworks/CloudSigner</li>
<li>/Library/Application Support/frameworks/Microsoft Excel</li>
<li>/Library/Application Support/frameworks/Hancom Office HWP</li>
<li>/Library/Application Support/frameworks/zoom.us</li>
<li>/Library/Application Support/loginitems/onedrive/com.onedrive.updater</li>
</ul>
<p>The payload loaded by the Nimcore loader has been identified as SneakMain.macOS, written in the Nim programming language. Upon execution, it reads its configuration from <code>/private/var/tmp/cfg</code>, which is likely created by the installer. The configuration&#8217;s original contents are recovered through RC4 decryption with the same key and base64 decoding. In the configuration, a C2 URL and machine ID (<code>mid</code>) are concatenated with the pipe character (&#8220;|&#8221;). Then SneakMain.macOS constructs a JSON object containing this information, along with additional fields such as the malware&#8217;s version, current time, and process list, which is then serialized and sent to the C2 server. The request includes the header <code>Content-Type: application/json</code>.</p>
<p>As a response, the malware receives additional AppleScript commands and uses the <code>osascript -e</code> command to execute them. If it fails to fetch the response, it tries to connect to a default C2 server every minute. There are two URLs hard-coded into the malware: <code>hxxps://file-server[.]store/update</code> and <code>hxxps://cloud-server[.]store/update</code>.</p>
<p>One interesting external component of this chain is the configuration updater. This updater verifies the presence of the configuration file and updates the C2 server address to <code>hxxps://flashserve[.]store/update</code> with the same encryption method, while preserving the existing mid value. Upon a successful update, it outputs the updated configuration to standard output.</p>
<p>Beside the Nim-based chain, we also identified a previous version of the SneakMain.macOS binary, written in Rust. This version only consists of a launcher and the Rust-based SneakMain. It is expected to create a corresponding plist for regular execution, but this has not yet been discovered. The Rust version supports two execution modes:</p>
<ul>
<li>With arguments: the malware uses the C2 server and <code>mid</code> as parameters</li>
<li>Without arguments: the malware loads an encrypted configuration file located at <code>/Library/Scripts/Folder Actions/Check.plist</code></li>
</ul>
<p>This version collects a process list only at a specific time during execution, without checking newly created or terminated processes. The collected list is then sent to the C2 server via a <code>POST</code> request to <code>hxxps://chkactive[.]online/update</code>, along with the current time (<code>uid</code>) and machine ID (<code>mid</code>), using the <code>Content-Type: application/json</code> header. Similarly, it uses the <code>osascript -e</code> command to execute commands received from the C2 server.</p>
<h3 id="downtroy-v2-chain">DownTroy v2 chain</h3>
<p>The DownTroy.macOS v2 infection chain is the latest variant, composed of four components, with the payload being an AppleScript and the rest written in Nim. It was already covered by <a href="https://www.sentinelone.com/labs/macos-nimdoor-dprk-threat-actors-target-web3-and-crypto-platforms-with-nim-based-malware/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">SentinelOne</a> under the name of &#8220;NimDoor&#8221;. The Nimcore loader in this chain masquerades as Google LLC, using an intentional typo by replacing the &#8220;l&#8221; (lowercase &#8220;L&#8221;) in &#8220;Goog<strong>l</strong>e LLC&#8221; with an &#8220;I&#8221; (uppercase &#8220;i&#8221;).</p>
<ul>
<li>Installer: the primary installer file named <code>"installer"</code>, written in Nim</li>
<li>Dropper: a dropper file named <code>"CoreKitAgent"</code>, written in Nim</li>
<li>Loader: an auxiliary loader file named <code>"GoogIe LLC"</code> and identified as Nimcore loader, written in Nim</li>
<li>Final payload: DownTroy.macOS, written in AppleScript</li>
</ul>
<p>The installer, which is likely downloaded and initiated by a prior malicious script, serves as the entry point for this process. The dropper receives an interrupt (SIGINT) or termination signal (SIGTERM) like in the DownTroy v1 chain, recreating the components on disk to recover them. Notably, while the previously described RooTroy and SneakMain chains do not have this recovery functionality, we have observed that they configure plist files to automatically execute the Nimcore loader after one hour if the process terminates, and they retain other components. This demonstrates how the actor strategically leverages DownTroy chains to operate more discreetly, highlighting some of the key differences between each chain.</p>
<p>The installer should be provided with one parameter and will exit if executed without it. It then copies <code>./CoreKitAgent</code> and <code>./GoogIe LLC</code> from the current location to <code>~/Library/CoreKit/CoreKitAgent</code> and <code>~/Library/Application Support/Google LLC/GoogIe LLC</code>, respectively. Inside of the installer, <code>com.google.update.plist</code> (the name of the plist) is hard-coded to establish persistence, which is later referenced by the dropper and loader. The installer then concatenates this value, the given parameter, and the dropper&#8217;s filename into a single string, separated by a pipe (&#8220;|&#8221;).</p>
<p><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27054540/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns19.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="aligncenter size-full wp-image-117896" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27054540/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns19.png" alt="" width="935" height="97" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27054540/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns19.png 935w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27054540/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns19-300x31.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27054540/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns19-768x80.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27054540/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns19-740x77.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27054540/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns19-800x83.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 935px) 100vw, 935px" /></a></p>
<p>This string is encrypted using the AES algorithm with a hard-coded key and IV, and the resulting encrypted data is then saved to the configuration file.</p>
<ul>
<li>Key: <code>5B77F83ECEFA0E32BA922F61C9EFFF7F755BA51A010DB844CA7E8AD3DB28650A</code></li>
<li>IV: <code>2B499EB3865A7EF17264D15252B7F73E</code></li>
<li>Configuration file path: <code>/private/tmp/.config</code></li>
</ul>
<p>It fulfills its function by ultimately executing the copied dropper located at <code>~/Library/CoreKit/CoreKitAgent</code>.</p>
<p>The dropper in the DownTroy v2 chain uses macOS&#8217;s <code>kqueue</code> alongside Nim&#8217;s <code>async</code> runtime to manage asynchronous control flow, similar to CosmicDoor, the Nimcore loader in the RooTroy chain, and the Nim version of SneakMain.macOS. The dropper monitors events via <code>kqueue</code>, and when an event is triggered, it resumes the corresponding async tasks through a state machine managed by Nim. The primary functionality is implemented in state 1 of the async state machine.</p>
<p>The dropper then reads the encrypted configuration from <code>/private/tmp/.config</code> and decrypts it using the AES algorithm with the hard-coded key and IV, which are identical to those used in the installer. By splitting the decrypted data with a &#8220;|&#8221;, it extracts the loader path, the plist path, and the parameter provided to the installer. Next, it reads all the contents of itself and the loader, and deletes them along with the plist file in order to erase any trace of their existence. When the dropper is terminated, a handler function is triggered that utilizes the previously read contents to recreate itself and the loader file. In addition, a hard-coded hex string is interpreted as ASCII text, and the decoded content is written to the plist file path obtained from the configuration.</p>
<p><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27054816/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns20.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="aligncenter size-full wp-image-117897" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27054816/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns20.png" alt="" width="868" height="796" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27054816/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns20.png 868w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27054816/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns20-300x275.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27054816/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns20-768x704.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27054816/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns20-382x350.png 382w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27054816/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns20-740x679.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27054816/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns20-305x280.png 305w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27054816/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns20-800x734.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 868px) 100vw, 868px" /></a></p>
<p>In the contents above, variables enclosed in %&#8217;s are replaced with different strings based on hard-coded values and configurations. Both authentication key variables are stored as encrypted strings with the same AES algorithm as used for the configuration.</p>
<ul>
<li><code>%label%</code> -&gt; <code>com.google.update</code></li>
<li><code>%server_auth_key%</code> -&gt; AES-encrypted selfpath (<code>~/Library/CoreKit/CoreKitAgent</code>)</li>
<li><code>%client_auth_key%</code> -&gt; AES-encrypted configuration</li>
<li><code>%program%</code> -&gt; loader path (<code>~/Library/Application Support/Google LLC/GoogIe LLC</code>)</li>
</ul>
<p>The core functionality of this loader is to generate an AppleScript file using a hard-coded hex string and save it as <code>.ses</code> in the same directory. The script, identified as DownTroy.macOS, is designed to download an additional malicious script from a C2 server. It is nearly identical to the one used in the DownTroy v1 chain, with the only differences being the updated C2 servers and the curl command option.</p>
<p>We have observed three variants of this chain, all of which ultimately deploy the DownTroy.macOS malware but communicate with different C2 servers. Variant 1 communicates with the same C2 server as the one configured in the DownTroy v1 chain, though it appears in a hex-encoded form.</p>
<table>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td></td>
<td><strong>Config path</strong></td>
<td><strong>C2 server</strong></td>
<td><strong>Curl command</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Variant 1</strong></td>
<td>/private/var/tmp/cfg</td>
<td>hxxps://bots[.]autoupdate[.]online:8080/test</td>
<td>curl &#8211;no-buffer -X POST -H</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Variant 2</strong></td>
<td>/private/tmp/.config</td>
<td>hxxps://writeup[.]live/test,<br />
hxxps://safeup[.]store/test</td>
<td>curl &#8211;connect-timeout 30 &#8211;max-time 60 &#8211;no-buffer -X POST -H</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Variant 3</strong></td>
<td>/private/tmp/.config</td>
<td>hxxps://api[.]clearit[.]sbs/test,<br />
hxxps://api[.]flashstore[.]sbs/test</td>
<td>curl &#8211;connect-timeout 30 &#8211;max-time 60 &#8211;no-buffer -X POST -H</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The configuration file path used by variant 1 is the same as that of SneakMain. This indicates that the actor transitioned from the SneakMain chain to the DownTroy chain while enhancing their tools, and this variant&#8217;s dropper is identified as an earlier version that reads the plist file directly.</p>
<h3 id="sysphon-chain">SysPhon chain</h3>
<p>Unlike other infection chains, the SysPhon chain incorporates an older set of malware: the lightweight version of RustBucket and the known SugarLoader. According to a blog post by <a href="https://fieldeffect.com/blog/zoom-doom-bluenoroff-call-opens-the-door" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Field Effect</a>, the actor deployed the lightweight version of RustBucket, which we dubbed &#8220;SysPhon&#8221;, alongside suspected SugarLoader malware and its loader, disguised as a legitimate Wi-Fi updater. Although we were unable to obtain the suspected SugarLoader malware sample or the final payloads, we believe with medium-low confidence that this chain is part of the same campaign by BlueNoroff. This assessment is based on the use of <code>icloud_helper</code> (a tool used for stealing user passwords) and the same initial infection vector as before: a fake Zoom link. It&#8217;s not surprising, as both malicious tools have already been attributed to BlueNoroff, indicating that the tools were adapted for the campaign.</p>
<p>Considering the parameters and behavior outlined in the blog post above, an AppleScript script deployed <code>icloud_helper</code> to collect the user&#8217;s password and simultaneously installed the SysPhon malware. The malware then downloaded SugarLoader, which connected to the C2 server and port pair specified as a parameter. This ultimately resulted in the download of a launcher to establish persistence. Given this execution flow and SugarLoader&#8217;s historical role in retrieving the <a href="https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/elastic-catches-dprk-passing-out-kandykorn" target="_blank" rel="noopener">KANDYKORN</a> malware, it is likely that the final payload in the chain would be KANDYKORN or another fully-featured backdoor.</p>
<p>SysPhon is a downloader written in C++ that functions similarly to the third component of the RustBucket malware, which was initially developed in Rust and later rewritten in Swift. In March 2024, an ELF version of the third component compatible with Linux was uploaded to a multi-scanner service. In November 2024, <a href="https://www.sentinelone.com/labs/bluenoroff-hidden-risk-threat-actor-targets-macs-with-fake-crypto-news-and-novel-persistence/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">SentinelOne</a> reported on SysPhon, noting that it is typically distributed via a parent downloader that opens a legitimate PDF related to cryptocurrency topics. Shortly after the report, a Go version of SysPhon was also uploaded to the same scanner service.</p>
<p>SysPhon requires a C2 server specified as a parameter to operate. When executed, it generates a 16-byte random ID and retrieves the host name. It then enters a loop to conduct system reconnaissance by executing a series of commands:</p>
<table>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><strong>Information to collect</strong></td>
<td><strong>Command</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>macOS version</td>
<td>sw_vers &#8211;ProductVersion</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Current timezone</td>
<td>date +%Z</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>macOS installation log (Update, package, etc)</td>
<td>grep &#8220;Install Succeeded&#8221; /var/log/install.log awk &#8216;{print $1, $2}&#8217;</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Hardware information</td>
<td>sysctl -n hw.model</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Process list</td>
<td>ps aux</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>System boot time</td>
<td>sysctl kern.boottime</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The results of these commands are then sent to the specified C2 server inside a POST request with the following User-Agent header: <code>mozilla/4.0</code> (<code>compatible; msie 8.0; windows nt 5.1; trident/4.0</code>). This User-Agent is the same as the one used in the Swift implementation of <a href="https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/DPRK-strikes-using-a-new-variant-of-rustbucket" target="_blank" rel="noopener">the RustBucket variant</a>.</p><!-- Urvanov Syntax Highlighter v2.9.0 -->

<!-- [Format Time: 0.0001 seconds] -->
<p>
After sending the system reconnaissance data to the C2 server, SysPhon waits for commands. It determines its next action by examining the first character of the response it receives. If the response begins with 0, SysPhon executes the binary payload; if it&#8217;s 1, the downloader exits.</p>
<h3 id="ai-powered-attack-strategy">AI-powered attack strategy</h3>
<p>While the video feeds for fake calls were recorded via the fabricated Zoom phishing pages the actor created, the profile images of meeting participants appear to have been sourced from job platforms or social media platforms such as LinkedIn, Crunchbase, or X. Interestingly, some of these images were enhanced with GPT-4o. Since <a href="https://help.openai.com/en/articles/8912793-c2pa-in-chatgpt-images" target="_blank" rel="noopener">OpenAI implemented the C2PA standard specification metadata</a> to identify the generated images as artificial, the images created via ChatGPT include metadata that indicates their synthetic origin, which is embedded in file formats such as PNGs.</p>
<div id="attachment_117898" style="width: 1610px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055339/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns21.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117898" class="size-full wp-image-117898" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055339/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns21.png" alt="EXIF metadata of images generated by GPT-4o" width="1600" height="841" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055339/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns21.png 1600w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055339/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns21-300x158.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055339/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns21-1024x538.png 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055339/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns21-768x404.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055339/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns21-1536x807.png 1536w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055339/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns21-666x350.png 666w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055339/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns21-740x389.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055339/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns21-533x280.png 533w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055339/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns21-800x421.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1600px) 100vw, 1600px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117898" class="wp-caption-text">EXIF metadata of images generated by GPT-4o</p></div>
<p>Among these were images whose filenames were set to the target&#8217;s name. This indicates the actor likely used the target&#8217;s publicly available profile image to generate a suitable profile for use alongside the recorded video. Furthermore, the inclusion of Zoom&#8217;s legitimate favicon image leads us to assess with medium-high confidence that the actor is leveraging AI for image enhancement.</p>
<div id="attachment_117899" style="width: 1610px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055432/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns22.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117899" class="size-full wp-image-117899" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055432/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns22.png" alt="Victim's profile image enhanced using GPT-4o" width="1600" height="633" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055432/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns22.png 1600w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055432/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns22-300x119.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055432/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns22-1024x405.png 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055432/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns22-768x304.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055432/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns22-1536x608.png 1536w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055432/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns22-885x350.png 885w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055432/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns22-740x293.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055432/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns22-708x280.png 708w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055432/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns22-800x317.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1600px) 100vw, 1600px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117899" class="wp-caption-text">Victim&#8217;s profile image enhanced using GPT-4o</p></div>
<p>In addition, the secrets stealer module of SilentSiphon, <code>secrets.sh</code>, includes several comment lines. One of them uses a checkmark emoticon to indicate archiving success, although the comment was related to the backup being completed. Since threat actors rarely use comments, especially emoticons, in malware intended for real attacks, we suggest that BlueNoroff uses generative AI to write malicious scripts similar to this module. We assume they likely requested a backup script rather than an exfiltration script.</p>
<div id="attachment_117900" style="width: 852px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055526/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns23.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117900" class="size-full wp-image-117900" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055526/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns23.png" alt="Comments that appear to be AI-generated in the secrets stealer module" width="842" height="650" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055526/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns23.png 842w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055526/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns23-300x232.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055526/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns23-768x593.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055526/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns23-453x350.png 453w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055526/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns23-740x571.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055526/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns23-363x280.png 363w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055526/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns23-800x618.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 842px) 100vw, 842px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117900" class="wp-caption-text">Comments that appear to be AI-generated in the secrets stealer module</p></div>
<h2 id="the-ghosthire-campaign">The GhostHire campaign</h2>
<p>The <strong>GhostHire</strong> campaign was less visible than GhostCall, but it also began as early as mid-2023, with its latest wave observed recently. It overlaps with the GhostCall campaign in terms of infrastructure and tools, but instead of using video calls, the threat actors pose as fake recruiters to target developers and engineers. The campaign is disguised as skill assessment to deliver malicious projects, exploiting Telegram bots and GitHub as delivery vehicles. Based on historical attack cases of this campaign, we assess with medium confidence that this attack flow involving Telegram and GitHub represents the latest phase, which started no later than April this year.</p>
<h3 id="initial-access">Initial access</h3>
<p>The actor initiates communication with the target directly on Telegram. Victims receive a message with a job offer along with a link to a LinkedIn profile that impersonates a senior recruiter at a financial services company based in the United States.</p>
<div id="attachment_117902" style="width: 838px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055803/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns24-1.jpeg" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117902" class="size-full wp-image-117902" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055803/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns24-1.jpeg" alt="Fake LinkedIn profile" width="828" height="657" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055803/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns24-1.jpeg 828w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055803/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns24-1-300x238.jpeg 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055803/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns24-1-768x609.jpeg 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055803/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns24-1-441x350.jpeg 441w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055803/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns24-1-740x587.jpeg 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055803/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns24-1-353x280.jpeg 353w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055803/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns24-1-800x635.jpeg 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 828px) 100vw, 828px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117902" class="wp-caption-text">Fake LinkedIn profile</p></div>
<p>We observed that the actor uses a Telegram Premium account to enhance their credibility by employing a custom emoji sticker featuring the company&#8217;s logo. They attempt to make the other party believe they are in contact with a legitimate representative.</p>
<div id="attachment_117903" style="width: 592px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055849/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns25.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117903" class="size-full wp-image-117903" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055849/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns25.png" alt="Fake Telegram account" width="582" height="760" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055849/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns25.png 582w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055849/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns25-230x300.png 230w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055849/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns25-268x350.png 268w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055849/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns25-214x280.png 214w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 582px) 100vw, 582px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117903" class="wp-caption-text">Fake Telegram account</p></div>
<p>During the investigation, we noticed suspicious changes made to the Telegram account, such as a shift from the earlier recruiter persona to impersonating individuals associated with a Web3 multi-gaming application. The actor even changed their Telegram handle to remove the previous connection.</p>
<div id="attachment_117904" style="width: 592px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055947/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns26.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117904" class="size-full wp-image-117904" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055947/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns26.png" alt="The same Telegram account changed to impersonate a Web3 company founder" width="582" height="760" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055947/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns26.png 582w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055947/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns26-230x300.png 230w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055947/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns26-268x350.png 268w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27055947/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns26-214x280.png 214w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 582px) 100vw, 582px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117904" class="wp-caption-text">The same Telegram account changed to impersonate a Web3 company founder</p></div>
<p>During the early stages of our research and ongoing monitoring of publicly available malicious repositories, we observed a <a href="https://nguyenhuythanh.com/posts/having-fun-with-a-scamming-crypto-job/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">blog post</a> published by a publicly cited target. In this post, the author shares their firsthand experience with a scam attempt involving the same malicious repositories we already identified. It provided us with valuable insight into how the group initiates contact with a target and progresses through a fake interview process.</p>
<p>Following up on initial communication, the actor adds the target to a user list for a Telegram bot, which displays the impersonated company&#8217;s logo and falsely claims to streamline technical assessments for candidates. The bot then sends the victim an archive file (ZIP) containing a coding assessment project, along with a strict deadline (often around 30 minutes) to pressure the target into quickly completing the task. This urgency increases the likelihood of the target executing the malicious content, leading to initial system compromise.</p>
<p>The project delivered through the ZIP file appears to be a legitimate DeFi-related project written in Go, aiming at routing cryptocurrency transactions across various protocols. The main project code relies on an external malicious dependency specified in the <code>go.mod</code> file, rather than embedding malicious code directly into the project&#8217;s own files. The external project is named <code>uniroute</code>. It was published in the official Go packages repository on April 9, 2025.</p>
<p>We had observed this same repository earlier in our investigation, prior to identifying the victim&#8217;s blog post, which later validated our findings. In addition to the Golang repository, we discovered a TypeScript-based repository uploaded to GitHub that has the same download function.</p>
<div id="attachment_117905" style="width: 926px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060242/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns27.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117905" class="size-full wp-image-117905" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060242/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns27.png" alt="Uniroute malicious package is referenced via go.mod in the DeFi-related project" width="916" height="536" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060242/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns27.png 916w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060242/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns27-300x176.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060242/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns27-768x449.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060242/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns27-598x350.png 598w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060242/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns27-740x433.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060242/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns27-479x280.png 479w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060242/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns27-800x468.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 916px) 100vw, 916px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117905" class="wp-caption-text">Uniroute malicious package is referenced via go.mod in the DeFi-related project</p></div>
<p>Upon execution of the project, the malicious package is imported, and the <code>GetUniRoute()</code> function is called during the initialization of the unirouter at the following path: <code>contracts/UniswapUniversalRouter.go</code>. This function call acts as the entry point for the malicious code.</p>
<div id="attachment_117906" style="width: 848px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060353/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns28.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117906" class="size-full wp-image-117906" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060353/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns28.png" alt="Entry point of malicious function" width="838" height="692" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060353/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns28.png 838w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060353/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns28-300x248.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060353/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns28-768x634.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060353/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns28-424x350.png 424w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060353/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns28-740x611.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060353/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns28-339x280.png 339w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060353/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns28-800x661.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 838px) 100vw, 838px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117906" class="wp-caption-text">Entry point of malicious function</p></div>
<h3Malicious Golang packages</h3>
<p>The malicious package consists of several files:</p><!-- Urvanov Syntax Highlighter v2.9.0 -->

<!-- [Format Time: 0.0001 seconds] -->
<p>
The main malicious logic is implemented in the following files:</p>
<ol>
<li><code>uniroute.go</code>: the main entry point</li>
<li><code>win.go</code>: Windows-specific malicious code</li>
<li><code>lin.go</code>: Linux-specific malicious code</li>
<li><code>dar.go</code>: macOS (Darwin)-specific malicious code</li>
</ol>
<p>The main entry point of the package includes a basic base64-encoded blob that is decoded to a URL hosting the second-stage payload: hxxps://download.datatabletemplate[.]xyz/account/register/id=8118555902061899&amp;secret=QwLoOZSDakFh.</p>
<div id="attachment_117907" style="width: 1074px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060654/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns29.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117907" class="size-full wp-image-117907" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060654/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns29.png" alt="Base64-encoded C2 URL in the malicious package" width="1064" height="488" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060654/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns29.png 1064w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060654/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns29-300x138.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060654/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns29-1024x470.png 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060654/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns29-768x352.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060654/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns29-763x350.png 763w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060654/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns29-740x339.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060654/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns29-610x280.png 610w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060654/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns29-800x367.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1064px) 100vw, 1064px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117907" class="wp-caption-text">Base64-encoded C2 URL in the malicious package</p></div>
<p>When the User-Agent of the running platform is detected, the corresponding payload is retrieved and executed. The package utilizes Go build tags to execute different code depending on the operating system.</p>
<ul>
<li>Windows (<code>win.go</code>). Downloads its payload to <code>%TEMP%\init.ps1</code> and performs anti-antivirus checks by looking for the presence of the 360 Security process. If the 360 antivirus is not detected, the malware generates an additional VBScript wrapper at <code>%TEMP%\init.vbs</code>. The PowerShell script is then covertly executed with a bypassed execution policy, without displaying any windows to the user.</li>
<li>Linux (<code>lin.go</code>). Downloads its payload to <code>/tmp/init</code> and runs it as a bash script with <code>nohup</code>, ensuring the process continues running even after the parent process terminates.</li>
<li>macOS (<code>dar.go</code>). Similarly to Linux, downloads its payload to <code>/tmp/init</code> and uses <code>osascript</code> with nohup to execute it.</li>
</ul>
<p>We used our <a href="https://www.kaspersky.com/open-source-feed?icid=gl_sl_post-link-sas-open-source-feed_sm-team_fb57efce5be5d86f" target="_blank" rel="noopener">open source package monitoring tool</a> to discover that the actor had published several malicious Go packages with behavior similar to <code>uniroute</code>. These packages are imported into repositories and executed within a specific section of the code.</p>
<table>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><strong>Package</strong></td>
<td><strong>Version</strong></td>
<td><strong>Published date</strong></td>
<td><strong>Role</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>sorttemplate</td>
<td>v1.1.1 ~ v1.1.5</td>
<td>Jun 11, 2024 ~ Apr 17, 2025</td>
<td>Malicious dependency</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>sort</td>
<td>v1.1.2 ~ v1.1.7</td>
<td>Nov 10, 2024 ~ Apr 17, 2025</td>
<td>Refers to the malicious sorttemplate</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>sorttemplate</td>

<td>Jan 10, 2025</td>
<td>Malicious dependency</td>
</tr>

<h3 id="malicious-typescript-project">Malicious TypeScript project</h3>
<p>Not only did we observe attacks involving malicious Golang packages, but we also identified a malicious Next.js project written in TypeScript and uploaded to GitHub. This project includes TypeScript source code for an NFT-related frontend task. The project acts in a similar fashion to the Golang ones, except that there is no dependency. Instead, a malicious TypeScript file within the project downloads the second-stage payload from a hardcoded URL.</p>
<div id="attachment_117908" style="width: 1610px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060937/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns30.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117908" class="size-full wp-image-117908" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060937/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns30.png" alt="Malicious TypeScript-based project" width="1600" height="265" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060937/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns30.png 1600w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060937/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns30-300x50.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060937/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns30-1024x170.png 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060937/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns30-768x127.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060937/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns30-1536x254.png 1536w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060937/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns30-740x123.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27060937/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns30-800x133.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1600px) 100vw, 1600px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117908" class="wp-caption-text">Malicious TypeScript-based project</p></div>
<p>The malicious behavior is implemented in <code>pages/api/hello.ts</code>, and the <code>hello</code> API is fetched by <code>NavBar.tsx</code> with <code>fetch('/api/hello')</code>.</p><!-- Urvanov Syntax Highlighter v2.9.0 -->

		<div id="urvanov-syntax-highlighter-690378cfa31da590595921" class="urvanov-syntax-highlighter-syntax crayon-theme-classic urvanov-syntax-highlighter-font-monaco urvanov-syntax-highlighter-os-pc print-yes notranslate" data-settings=" minimize scroll-mouseover" style=" margin-top: 12px; margin-bottom: 12px; font-size: 12px !important; line-height: 15px !important;">

<!-- [Format Time: 0.0002 seconds] -->
<p>
We have to point out that this tactic isn&#8217;t unique to BlueNoroff. <a href="https://securelist.com/lazarus-new-malware/115059/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">The Lazarus group was the first</a> to adopt it, and the <a href="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/two-campaigns-by-north-korea-bad-actors-target-job-hunters/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Contagious Interview campaign</a> also uses it. However, the GhostHire campaign stands apart because it uses a completely different set of malware chains.</p>
<h3 id="downtroy-multi-platform-downloader">DownTroy: multi-platform downloader</h3>
<p>Upon accessing the URL with the correct User-Agent, corresponding scripts are downloaded for each OS: PowerShell for Windows, bash script for Linux, and AppleScript for macOS, which all turned out to be the DownTroy malware. It is the same as the final payload in the DownTroy chains from the GhostCall campaign and has been expanded to include versions for both Windows and Linux. In the GhostHire campaign, this script serves as the initial downloader, fetching various malware chains from a file hosting server.</p>
<div id="attachment_117931" style="width: 1610px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071356/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns31.jpg" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117931" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071356/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns31.jpg" alt="DownTroy delivery process" width="1600" height="712" class="size-full wp-image-117931" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071356/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns31.jpg 1600w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071356/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns31-300x134.jpg 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071356/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns31-1024x456.jpg 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071356/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns31-768x342.jpg 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071356/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns31-1536x684.jpg 1536w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071356/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns31-787x350.jpg 787w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071356/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns31-740x329.jpg 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071356/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns31-629x280.jpg 629w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071356/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns31-800x356.jpg 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1600px) 100vw, 1600px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117931" class="wp-caption-text">DownTroy delivery process</p></div>
<p>Over the course of tracking this campaign, we have observed multiple gradual updates to these DownTroy scripts. The final version shows that the PowerShell code is XOR-encrypted, and the AppleScript has strings split by individual characters. Additionally, all three DownTroy strains collect comprehensive system information including OS details, domain name, host name, username, proxy settings, and VM detection alongside process lists.</p>
<h3 id="full-infection-chain-on-windows">Full infection chain on Windows</h3>
<p>In January 2025, we identified a victim who had executed a malicious TypeScript project located at &lt;<code>company name&gt;-wallet-portfolio</code>, which followed the recruiter persona from the financial company scenario described earlier. The subsequent execution of the malicious script created the files <code>init.vbs</code> and <code>init.ps1</code> in the <code>%temp%</code> directory.</p>
<p>The DownTroy script (<code>init.ps1</code>) was running to download additional malware from an external server every 30 seconds. During the attack, two additional script files, <code>chsplitobf.ps1</code> and <code>sinst.bat</code>, were downloaded and executed on the infected host. Though we weren&#8217;t able to obtain the files, based on our detection, we assess the PowerShell script harvests credentials stored in a browser, similar to SilentSiphon on macOS.</p>
<p>In addition, in the course of the attack, several other payloads written in Go and Rust rather than scripts, were retrieved from the file hosting server <code>dataupload[.]store</code> and executed.</p>
<div id="attachment_117932" style="width: 1610px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071408/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns32.jpg" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117932" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071408/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns32.jpg" alt="Overall Windows infection chain" width="1600" height="737" class="size-full wp-image-117932" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071408/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns32.jpg 1600w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071408/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns32-300x138.jpg 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071408/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns32-1024x472.jpg 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071408/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns32-768x354.jpg 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071408/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns32-1536x708.jpg 1536w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071408/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns32-760x350.jpg 760w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071408/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns32-740x341.jpg 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071408/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns32-608x280.jpg 608w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071408/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns32-800x369.jpg 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1600px) 100vw, 1600px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117932" class="wp-caption-text">Overall Windows infection chain</p></div>
<h3 id="new-method-for-payload-delivery">New method for payload delivery</h3>
<p>In contrast to GhostCall, DownTroy.Windows would retrieve a base64-encoded binary blob from the file hosting server and inject it into the <code>cmd.exe</code> process after decoding. This blob typically consists of metadata, a payload, and the loader code responsible for loading the payload. The first five bytes of the blob represent a <code>CALL</code> instruction that invokes the loader code, followed by <code>0x48</code> bytes of metadata. The loader, which is 0xD6B bytes in size, utilizes the metadata to load the payload into memory. The payload is written to newly allocated space, then relocated, and its import address table (IAT) is rebuilt from the same metadata. Finally, the payload is executed with the <code>CreateThread</code> function.</p>
<div id="attachment_117925" style="width: 1534px" class="wp-caption aligncenter"><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071245/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns33.jpg" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-117925" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071245/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns33.jpg" alt="Binary blob structure" width="1524" height="852" class="size-full wp-image-117925" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071245/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns33.jpg 1524w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071245/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns33-300x168.jpg 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071245/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns33-1024x572.jpg 1024w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071245/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns33-768x429.jpg 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071245/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns33-270x150.jpg 270w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071245/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns33-626x350.jpg 626w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071245/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns33-740x414.jpg 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071245/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns33-501x280.jpg 501w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/28071245/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns33-800x447.jpg 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1524px) 100vw, 1524px" /></a><p id="caption-attachment-117925" class="wp-caption-text">Binary blob structure</p></div>
<p>The metadata contains some of the fields from PE file format, such as an entry point of the payload, imagebase, number of sections, etc, needed to dynamically load the payload. The payload is invoked by the loader by referencing the metadata stored separately, so it has a corrupted COFF header when loaded. Generally, payloads in PE file format should have a legitimate header with the corresponding fields, but in this case, the top <code>0x188</code> bytes of the PE header of the payload are all filled with dummy values, making it difficult to analyze and detect.</p>
<h3 id="uac-bypass">UAC bypass</h3>
<p>We observed that the first thing the actor deployed after DownTroy was installed was the User Account Control (UAC) bypass tool. The first binary blob fetched by DownTroy contained the payload bypassing UAC that used a technique disclosed in 2019 by the <a href="https://googleprojectzero.blogspot.com/2019/12/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Google Project Zero</a> team. This RPC-based UAC bypass leveraging the <code>201ef99a-7fa0-444c-9399-19ba84f12a1a</code> interface was also observed in the <a href="https://www.threatdown.com/blog/new-variant-of-konni-malware-used-in-campaign-targetting-russia/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">KONNI malware execution chain in 2021</a>. However, the process that obtains the privilege had been changed from <code>Taskmgr.exe</code> to <code>Computerdefaults.exe</code>.</p>
<p>The commands executed through this technique are shown below. In this case, <code>this.exe</code> is replaced by the legitimate <code>explorer.exe</code> due to parent PID spoofing.</p>
<p><a href="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27061938/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns34.png" class="magnificImage"><img loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="aligncenter size-full wp-image-117912" src="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27061938/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns34.png" alt="" width="935" height="286" srcset="https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27061938/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns34.png 935w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27061938/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns34-300x92.png 300w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27061938/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns34-768x235.png 768w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27061938/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns34-740x226.png 740w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27061938/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns34-915x280.png 915w, https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2025/10/27061938/bluenoroff-apt-campaigns34-800x245.png 800w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 935px) 100vw, 935px" /></a></p>
<p>In other words, the actor was able to run DownTroy with elevated privileges, which is the starting point for all further actions. It also executed <code>init.vbs</code>, the launcher that runs DownTroy, with elevated privileges.</p>
<h3 id="rootroy-windows-in-go">RooTroy.Windows in Go</h3>
<p>RooTroy.Windows is the first non-scripted malware installed on an infected host. It is a simple downloader written in Go, same to the malware used in the GhostCall campaign. Based on our analysis of RooTroy&#8217;s behavior and execution flow, it was loaded and executed by a Windows service named <code>NetCheckSvc</code>.</p>
<p>Although we did not obtain the command or installer used to register the <code>NetCheckSvc</code> service, we observed that the installer had been downloaded from <code>dataupload[.]store</code> via DownTroy and injected into the legitimate <code>cmd.exe</code> process with the parameter <code>-m yuqqm2ced6zb9zfzvu3quxtrz885cdoh</code>. The installer then probably created the file <code>netchksvc.dll</code> at <code>C:\Windows\system32</code> and configured it to run as a service named <code>NetCheckSvc</code>. Once <code>netchksvc.dll</code> was executed, it loaded RooTroy into memory, which allowed it to operate in the memory of the legitimate <code>svchost.exe</code> process used to run services in Windows.</p>
<p>RooTroy.Windows initially retrieves configuration information from the file <code>C:\Windows\system32\smss.dat</code>. The contents of this file are decrypted using RC4 with a hardcoded key: <code>B3CC15C1033DE79024F9CF3CD6A6A7A9B7E54A1A57D3156036F5C05F541694B7</code>. This key is different from the one used in the macOS variant of this malware, but the same C2 URLs were used in the GhostCall campaign: <code>readysafe[.]xyz</code> and <code>safefor[.]xyz</code>.</p>
<p>Then <code>RooTroy.Windows</code> creates a string object <code>{"rt": "5.0.0"}</code>, which is intended to represent the malware&#8217;s version. This string is encrypted using RC4 with another hardcoded string, <code>C4DB903322D17C8CBF1D1DB55124854C0B070D6ECE54162B6A4D06DF24C572DF</code>. It is the same as the key used in <code>RooTroy.macOS</code>, and it is stored at <code>C:\ProgramData\Google\Chrome\version.dat</code>.</p>
<p>Next, the malware collects device information, including lists of current, new and terminated processes, OS information, boot time, and more, which are all structured in a JSON object. It then sends the collected data to the C2 server using the POST method with the <code>Content-Type: application/json</code> header.</p>
<p>The response is parsed into a JSON object to extract additional information required for executing the actual command. The commands are executed based on the value of the <code>type</code> field in the response, with each command processing its corresponding parameters in the required object.</p>
<table>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><strong>Value of </strong><strong>type</strong></td>
<td><strong>Description</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>0</td>
<td>Send current configuration to C2</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>1</td>
<td>Update received configuration with the configuration file (<code>smss.dat</code>)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>2</td>
<td>Payload injection</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>3</td>
<td>Self-update</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>If the received value of <code>type</code> is <code>2</code> or <code>3</code>, the responses include a common <code>source</code> field within the parsed <code>data</code>, indicating where the additional payload originates. Depending on the value of <code>source</code>, the data field in the parsed data contains either the file path where the payload is stored on the disk, the C2 server address from which it should be downloaded, or the payload itself encoded in base64. Additionally, if the <code>cipher</code> field is set to <code>true</code>, the key field is used as the RC4 decryption key.</p>
<table>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><strong>Value of </strong><strong>source</strong></td>
<td><strong>Description</strong></td>
<td><strong>Value of </strong><strong>data</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>0</td>
<td>Read payload from a specific file</td>
<td>File path</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>1</td>
<td>Fetch payload from another server</td>
<td>C2 address</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>2</td>
<td>Delivered by the current JSON object</td>
<td>base64-encoded payload</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>If the value of <code>type</code> is set to <code>2</code>, the injection mode, referred to as <code>peshooter</code> in the code, is activated to execute an additional payload into memory. This mode checks whether the payload sourced from the data field is encrypted by examining the <code>cipher</code> value as a flag in the parsed data. If it is, the payload is decrypted with the RC4 algorithm. If no key is provided in the key value, a hardcoded string, <code>A6C1A7CE43B029A1EF4AE69B26F745440ECCE8368C89F11AC999D4ED04A31572</code>, is used as the default key.</p>
<p>If the <code>pid</code> value is not specified (e.g., set to -1), the process with the name provided in the <code>process</code> field is executed in suspended mode, with the optional <code>argument</code> value as its input. Additionally, if a <code>sid</code> value is provided at runtime, a process with the corresponding session ID is created. If a <code>pid</code> value is explicitly given, the injection is performed into that specific process.</p>
<p>Before performing the injection, the malware enables the <code>SeDebugPrivilege</code> privilege for process injection and unhooks the loaded <code>ntdll.dll</code> for the purpose of bypassing detection. This is a DLL unhooking technique that dynamically loads and patches the <code>.text</code> section of <code>ntdll.dll</code> to bypass the hooking of key functions by security software to detect malicious behavior.</p>
<p>Once all the above preparations are completed, the malware finally injects the payload into the targeted process.</p>
<p>If the value of type is set to 3, self-update mode is activated. Similar to injection mode, it first checks whether the payload sourced from the data is encrypted and, if so, decrypts it using RC4 with a hardcoded key: <code>B494A0AE421AFE170F6CB9DE2C1193A78FBE16F627F85139676AFC5D9BFE93A2</code>. A random 32-byte string is then generated, and the payload is encrypted using RC4 with this string as the key. The encrypted payload is stored in the file located at <code>C:\Windows\system32\boot.sdl</code>, while the generated random key is saved unencrypted in <code>C:\Windows\system32\wizard.sep</code>. This means the loader will read the wizard.sep file to retrieve the RC4 key, use it to decrypt the payload from <code>boot.sdl</code>, and then load it.</p>
<p>After successfully completing the update operation, the following commands are created under the filename <code>update-[random].bat</code> in the <code>%temp%</code> directory:</p><!-- Urvanov Syntax Highlighter v2.9.0 -->

<p>
This batch script restarts a service called <code>NetCheckSvc</code> and self-deletes, which causes the loader <code>netchksvc.dll</code> to be reloaded. In other words, the self-update mode updates RooTroy itself by modifying two files mentioned above.</p>
<p>According to our telemetry, we observed that the payload called RealTimeTroy was fetched by RooTroy and injected into <code>cmd.exe</code>  process with the injected <code>wss://signsafe[.]xyz/update</code> parameter.</p>
<h3 id="realtimetroy-in-go">RealTimeTroy in Go</h3>
<p>The backdoor requires at least two arguments: a simple string and a C2 server address. Before connecting to the given C2 server, the first argument is encrypted using the RC4 algorithm with the key <code>9939065709AD8489E589D52003D707CBD33AC81DC78BC742AA6E3E811BA344C</code> and then base64 encoded. In the observed instance, this encoded value is passed to the &#8220;p&#8221; (payload) field in the request packet.</p>
<p>The entire request packet is additionally encrypted using RC4 algorithm with the key <code>4451EE8BC53EA7C148D8348BC7B82ACA9977BDD31C0156DFE25C4A879A1D2190</code>. RealTimeTroy then sends this encrypted message to the C2 server to continue communication and receive commands from the C2.</p>
<p>Then the malware receives a response from the C2. The value of &#8220;e&#8221; (event) within the response should be 5, and the value of &#8220;p&#8221; is decoded using base64 and then decrypted using RC4 with the key <code>71B743C529F0B27735F7774A0903CB908EDC93423B60FE9BE49A3729982D0E8D</code>, which is deserialized in JSON. The command is extracted from the &#8220;c&#8221; (command) field in the JSON object, and the malware performs specific actions according to the command.</p>
<table>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><strong>Command</strong></td>
<td><strong>Description</strong></td>
<td><strong>Parameter from C2</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>1</td>
<td>Get list of subfiles</td>
<td>Directory path</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>2</td>
<td>Wipe file</td>
<td>File path</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>3</td>
<td>Read file</td>
<td>File path</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>4</td>
<td>Read directory</td>
<td>Directory path</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>5</td>
<td>Get directory information</td>
<td>Directory path</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>6</td>
<td>Get process information</td>
<td>&#8211;</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>7</td>
<td>Terminate process</td>
<td>Process ID</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>8</td>
<td>Execute command</td>
<td>Command line</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>10</td>

</tr>
<tr>
<td>11</td>
<td>Change work directory</td>
<td>Directory path</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>12</td>
<td>Get device information</td>
<td>&#8211;</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>13</td>
